Continuum honeycomb Schrödinger operators with incommensurate line defects
P. Amenoagbadji, M. I. Weinstein

Abstract
. We study wave propagation in 2D honeycomb structures with a non-commensurate or “irrational” line defect or edge. Our model is a Schrödinger operator which interpolates, across the edge, between two distinct bulk (asymptotic) Hamiltonians with a common spectral gap about the “Dirac point” of an unperturbed honeycomb operator. We seek edge states , eigenstates that are bounded and oscillatory parallel to the edge, and decaying in the transverse direction. For non-commensurate edges, the rigorous definition of these states is nontrivial due to the lack of translation invariance along the edge. To address this, we exploit quasiperiodicity along the edge by expressing the Hamiltonian as the restriction of a 3D (degenerate elliptic) Hamiltonian describing a 3D medium with a 2D interface within which there is periodicity. Via multiscale analysis, we construct approximate edge states in this 3D setting and obtain by restriction 2D edge states which are quasiperiodic along the irrational edge. These edge states are seeded by eigenfunctions of an effective Dirac operator, which has an infinite block-diagonal structure due to the non-commensurate geometry. A consequence is that infinitely many edge state eigenpairs arise, whose energies are dense in the perturbed bulk spectral gap. In a forthcoming paper, we rigorously construct these gap-filling edge states under a Diophantine condition. The main result here is a key tool in this construction: a resolvent expansion for the 3D Hamiltonian, whose leading term is the resolvent of the block-diagonal Dirac operator. The validity of this expansion requires an omnidirectional non-resonance ( no-fold ) condition on the dispersion functions of the unperturbed honeycomb Hamiltonian. This condition is satisfied in the strong binding regime. In contrast with earlier works on commensurate edges, the omnidirectional condition is independent of the edge.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview

The study of honeycomb materials in condensed matter physics was sparked by the experimental isolation of graphene, a crystalline two-dimensional hexagonal arrangement of carbon atoms extending to a macroscale
[
70
,
122
]
. The remarkable properties of quantum electronic waves in graphene have catalyzed investigations of wave propagation in diverse engineered two-dimensional structures with hexagonal and other novel symmetries. These so-called
artificial graphene
systems have been studied and realized experimentally, for instance, in electronics
[
142
]
, photonics
[
125
,
101
,
130
,
131
,
16
,
151
,
123
,
82
,
14
]
, acoustics
[
146
,
100
,
118
,
152
]
, and mechanics
[
119
]
; see also the survey
[
135
]
for further references.

Many of the interesting properties of graphene-like materials are related to the presence of
Dirac points
in their Floquet-Bloch band structure (Section
2
), which encodes the spectral properties of an underlying periodic Hamiltonian, and hence wave propagation properties of the system. A Dirac point is an energy/quasi-momentum pair,
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
, at which two consecutive dispersion surfaces touch conically. For Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}^{0}
governing graphene-like structures, the existence of Dirac points is rooted in the inversion, complex conjugation, and rotational symmetries of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
; see Section
2.4
. Concerning their stability, roughly speaking, Dirac points persist under small perturbations of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
that break rotational invariance (the dispersion cones merely distort), but breaking either inversion or complex conjugation symmetry induces a local energy gap around the Dirac point.

In this paper, we study a class of continuum Schrödinger Hamiltonians introduced in
[
61
]
, building on ideas of
[
83
,
129
]
, for realizing quantum Hall-like edge states in photonic crystals. Further mathematical investigations appear in
[
46
,
111
,
43
]
, and related work on square and deformed square lattices can be found in
[
31
,
30
]
. Our Hamiltonian interpolates across a
line defect
or
edge
, via a domain wall, between two distinct bulk (asymptotic) perturbed honeycomb Hamiltonians, as illustrated in Figure
1
. These asymptotic Hamiltonians are designed to be insulating (“gapped”) about the Dirac energy,
E_{D}
(Sections
3.1
and
6.2
).

When the line defect is oriented in the direction of a period lattice vector, we say the line defect is commensurate or “rational”.
The Hamiltonian describing the medium with an edge is translation-invariant along the edge, and this edge Hamiltonian can be studied using Floquet-Bloch theory. This edge Hamiltonian admits
edge states
: time-harmonic states which are pseudo-periodic (propagating) in the direction parallel to the line defect, and decaying in the transverse direction
[
61
,
111
,
46
,
43
]
. The energies of such edge states lie along curves in the bulk band gap, which are parameterized by a (scalar) parallel quasi-momentum. An algebraic count of the number of gap-traversing curves (spectral flow) is equal to the difference of bulk Chern numbers, integer-valued topological indices determined by the asymptotic
bulk
band structures. In cases where complex conjugation symmetry is broken, this difference may be non-zero, ensuring the existence of gap traversing edge state curves. Spectral flow is also related to a notion of quantum conductivity. The corresponding edge states are said to be
topologically protected
since a relatively compact perturbation of the Hamiltonian (even large) does not change topological indices which depend only on the bulk. This is a manifestation of the bulk-edge correspondence principle; see for example,
[
86
,
51
,
52
,
85
,
78
,
45
,
44
]
for discrete models, and in
[
11
,
43
,
76
,
47
,
48
,
136
,
128
,
12
,
13
]
for continuous Hamiltonians.

Figure 1
.
The Hamiltonian studied in this paper models a smooth transition, via a domain wall, between two distinct bulk honeycomb Hamiltonians, across an
edge
. If the edge is aligned with the period lattice, then it is said to be
commensurate
(left panel); otherwise, it is
non-commensurate
(right panel).

In this paper, we initiate the study of edge states in cases where the line defect lies in a direction which is
not
a period lattice vector. In this case, the line defect is said to be non-commensurate or “irrational”. The main difficulty is a complete lack of translation invariance of the two-dimensional medium, which prevents the use of the Floquet-Bloch theory.

We propose a framework for the rigorous definition of edge states in systems which are non-commensurate. Our approach relies on the observation that the Hamiltonian is
quasiperiodic
along the edge (see Remark
3.2
). More precisely, it is realized as the restriction, to a two-dimensional plane, of a three-dimensional Hamiltonian, governing a material which interpolates between three-dimensional bulk periodic structures across a planar interface, within which there is translation invariance. This observation has been used in the homogenization theory of periodic elliptic boundary value problems in presence of a sharp termination
[
72
,
73
]
or an interface
[
20
]
. It has also been exploited in
[
3
]
to numerically solve the Helmholtz equation in a similar setting.

Using this observation, in this paper:

(a).

We introduce a general notion of edge states for the original two-dimensional medium by defining them as restrictions of three-dimensional (augmented) edge states. These augmented edge states are nontrivial solutions of a spectral problem in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
which are pseudo-periodic along the directions of a two-dimensional hyperplane (although determined by a single parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}\in[-\pi,\pi]
) and decaying in the direction transverse to this hyperplane; see further discussion in Section
1.3.1
. In this lifted or augmented setting, the underlying Hamiltonian is degenerate elliptic on a three-dimensional cylinder. This approach recovers the known pseudo-periodic edge states for commensurate media, but allows for edge states with more complex (
quasiperiodic
) behavior along non-commensurate edges.

(b).

In our augmented Hamiltonian, the length scale of the transition between asymptotic bulk media across the line defect is large compared with the scale of periodicity. Via multiple scale analysis, we exploit this scale separation, to construct approximate edge states which are now quasiperiodic along the edge, and decaying transverse to the edge. In the commensurate case, for a fixed
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}
, edge states are seeded by one or two effective Dirac operators (associated with the two independent high-symmetry quasi-momenta). In contrast, for the non-commensurate case, we show that an
infinite
family of effective Dirac operators participates in seeding approximate edge states whose energies are dense in the bulk gap.

(c).

An understanding of the spectral properties of our edge Hamiltonian (original and augmented) is naturally obtained by a study of the resolvent. Our main result, Theorem
7.1
, is an asymptotic expansion for the centered and scaled resolvent of the three-dimensional elliptically degenerate operator for energies near the Dirac energy; see Section
1.3.3
. For non-commensurate edges, this asymptotic expansion involves, for each fixed parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}\in[-\pi,\pi]
, a
countable
set of effective Dirac operators.
In our proof, we extend the approach developed in
[
46
,
40
,
43
]
. The resolvent expansion (see (
1.2
) and (
7.13
)) shows that a “blowup” to order one of a
\mathcal{O}(\delta)
–neighborhood of the bulk energy gap is characterized by resolvents of these effective Dirac operators.

A crucial assumption for our analysis is an
omnidirectional
no-fold condition on the unperturbed honeycomb operator
\mathcal{H}^{0}
; we require that the dispersion surfaces associated with the unperturbed honeycomb model attain the Dirac energy only at the vertices of the Brillouin zone. Previous works for commensurate edges
[
61
,
111
,
46
,
40
]
only required a weaker condition, which depends on the choice of commensurate edge. This more restrictive omnidirectional no-fold condition holds for instance for honeycomb Schrödinger operators in the strong binding regime
[
60
]
. If
\mathcal{H}^{0}
satisfies the omnidirectional no-fold condition, then our edge Hamiltonian interpolates between two bulk Hamiltonians which are spectrally gapped about the Dirac energy,
E_{D}
.

The resolvent expansion of Theorem
7.1
, for non-commensurate systems, plays a key role in forthcoming work
[
4
]
, where we complete the program discussed above to rigorously construct genuine quasiperiodic edge states for irrational edges. Assuming a Diophantine condition on the irrational parameter which sets the orientation of the edge, we show that near the Dirac energy, the spectrum associated with the augmented three-dimensional problem in a cylinder consists of a dense set of eigenvalues. Moreover, the associated eigenfunctions can be restricted to the original two-dimensional hyperplane, hence giving rise to two-dimensional edge states with quasiperiodic behavior along the edge.

Finally, let us mention that the so-called lifting approach described in Part
(a)
is intrinsically linked to the study of quasicrystals
[
137
]
, which are crystals with no periodicity directions. The connection between quasicrystals and quasiperiodicity was originally established by
[
36
,
104
]
, and has led to the cut-and-project method
[
50
,
97
,
55
]
, where quasicrystals are modeled as restrictions of higher dimensional (periodic) crystals. Mathematical contributions to the homogenization of quasicrystals and quasiperiodic structures using the cut-and-project approach (or its continuous counterpart, the lifting approach), can be found in
[
103
,
23
,
149
,
66
]
. Numerical studies of electronic, electromagnetic, or water wave propagation in non-commensurate systems based on the lifting approach appear in
[
133
,
150
,
2
,
121
,
147
,
120
,
93
]
. The spectral properties of quasiperiodic structures are known to be very subtle. A well-known example which reflects this complexity is given by the almost Mathieu operator
-\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}}+2{\lambda}\cos(2\pi\mskip 1.5mu\alpha\mskip 1.5mun+x)
, which models an electron in a magnetic field with constant flux
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
on a lattice. The spectrum of this operator, for
\alpha
irrational, was conjectured
[
10
]
to be a Cantor set (that is, a closed set with no isolated points, and whose closure has an empty interior). This conjecture was proven in
[
7
]
. For a similar result in graphene, see
[
15
]
. Related contributions can be found for instance in
[
80
,
17
,
140
,
28
,
144
,
53
,
95
,
24
,
25
,
102
,
8
,
34
,
6
,
94
]
for discrete models, and in
[
38
,
134
,
116
,
141
,
69
,
54
]
for continuous models.

1.2.
Mathematical framework and previous analytical work

The equilateral lattice
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
and its dual lattice
\Lambda^{*}:=\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
are defined in (
1.3
), and are such that
{\mathbf{k}}_{n}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{l}=\delta_{nl}
. The Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
, a particular choice of fundamental cell for
\Lambda^{*}
, is a regular hexagon shown in Figure
2
. Its vertices consist of the points
{\mathbf{K}}
,
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}=-{\mathbf{K}}
, and their images under rotation by
2\pi/3
.

The edge Hamiltonian we study is constructed in several steps:

•

The starting point is the Schrödinger operator
\mathcal{H}^{0}=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}})
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where
V
is a
honeycomb lattice potential
,
i.e.

V
is smooth,
\Lambda
–periodic, even (inversion symmetry), real-valued (complex conjugation symmetry), and invariant under clockwise rotation by
2\pi/3
; see Definition
2.1
. Two examples are a potential,
V
, which is a sum of translates of sufficiently decaying radially symmetric “atomic” potential wells, where the sum is taken over the honeycomb lattice (a graphene-like potential) or the triangular lattice. Generic honeycomb lattice Schrödinger operators,
\mathcal{H}^{0}
, have Dirac points in their band structure. These are energy/quasi-momentum pairs
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
, at which two dispersion surfaces touch conically over the vertices
{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}
of the Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}

[
62
,
64
]
; see Section
2.5
. See
[
18
]
for a group representation perspective, and
[
49
]
for a discussion of why conical degeneracies are the generic type of degeneracy in Hermitian Hamiltonians.
A manifestation of this phenomenon is the emergence of conical degeneracies, when a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
–lattice potential with quadratic degeneracies is slightly deformed; see
[
99
,
31
]
.
The existence of Dirac points was studied as well for
\|V\|_{\infty}
small in
[
81
,
32
]
, when
V
is a superposition of Dirac delta distributions in
[
110
]
, and in the strong binding regime in
[
60
]
. Related results hold for divergence-form elliptic operators
[
5
,
29
,
111
,
113
]
, graph Laplacians
[
106
]
, and continuum Laplacians on graph-like domains
[
37
]
, all exhibiting honeycomb symmetries. A general framework for the study of band dispersion degeneracies in
n
-dimensional lattices can be found in
[
41
]
.

•

Following
[
83
,
129
]
, we perturb
\mathcal{H}^{0}
with a
magneto-optic
term (see
[
151
,
148
]
and
[
111
, Appendix A.4]
), to obtain the operator
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}=\mathcal{H}^{0}+\delta\,\nabla\cdot a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}\,\nabla
, where
\sigma_{2}=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)
is the standard
2\times 2
Pauli matrix, and
{\bm{x}}\mapsto a({\bm{x}})
is smooth,
\Lambda
–periodic, even, and real-valued; see Section
3.1
for the precise assumptions on
a({\bm{x}})
. Because it breaks complex conjugation symmetry, for
\delta
small enough, the perturbation induced by
a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}
opens a local gap around the Dirac energy, i.e. a gap for quasi-momentum/energy pairs located
near the Dirac points
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
.

•

Finally, we slowly interpolate between bulk operators
\mathcal{H}^{-\delta}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}
using a smooth
domain wall function

\zeta\mapsto\kappa(\zeta)
, where
\kappa(\zeta)\to\pm 1
as
\zeta\to\pm\infty
. The interpolation is taken to be in a direction transverse to an edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
, where
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}+r\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, with
r\in\mathbb{R}
. The corresponding operator is formally given by

\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}:=\mathcal{H}^{0}+\delta\,\nabla\cdot\kappa(\delta{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}})\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}\,\nabla,

where
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}=-r\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2}
is such that
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=0
. The operator
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
asymptotes to two bulk operators

\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}\,u({\bm{x}})\to\mathcal{H}^{\pm\delta}\,u({\bm{x}}),\quad\textrm{ as}\quad{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}}\to\pm\infty.

We call the edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}

rational
(commensurate) if
r\in\mathbb{Q}
; otherwise,
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
is
irrational
(non-commensurate).

A schematic representation of the junction encoded by
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
is given in Figure
1
for
r=-1
(left panel) and
r=-\sqrt{2}/2
(right panel).

Formally, an edge state with energy
E\in\mathbb{R}
is a nontrivial solution of the eigenvalue problem
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}\,\Psi=E\,\Psi
such that
\Psi({\bm{x}})\to 0
as
|{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}}|\to\infty
and
\Psi({\bm{x}})
exhibits “
plane wave-like
” behavior along the edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
. The precise nature of this plane wave-like behavior is determined by the invariance properties of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
in the
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
–direction. In fact, if the edge parameter is rational,
i.e.

r=b_{1}/a_{1}
where
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
are coprime integers, then
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
is invariant under the translation
{\bm{x}}\mapsto{\bm{x}}+a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
. Associated to this translation invariance is a parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}\in[-\pi,\pi]
, and the plane wave-like behavior corresponds to
pseudo-periodicity
:
\Psi({\bm{x}}+a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathfrak{v}}_{1})=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\,k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}\,\Psi({\bm{x}})
. Such solutions are called
edge states with parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}
. For
\delta
small, the bifurcation of edge states from the Dirac point has been established in
[
61
,
111
,
46
,
43
]
using a Lyapunov-Schmidt/Schur complement reduction procedure. Sharp junctions,
i.e.
discontinuous transitions between bulk media (realized for instance using
\kappa(\zeta)=\sign(\zeta)
) were investigated in
[
5
,
113
]
using an integral equation-formulation of the edge state problem and a generalized Rouché theorem. Similar bifurcation results were derived for one-dimensional systems
[
59
,
64
,
40
]
, periodic waveguides
[
126
]
, and square lattice potentials
[
30
,
127
]
; we also refer to
[
65
,
37
]
for related results in different perturbation settings (strong binding regime or thin graph-like domains), and to
[
57
,
58
,
78
,
75
]
for non-perturbative results in discrete models.

In contrast, little seems to be known for irrational edges. Since
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
is not translation-invariant along the edge, the notion of the plane wave-like behavior of edge states requires a careful formulation. The results
[
88
,
89
,
77
]
, for models of periodic media with sharp terminations or junctions between two periodic media, suggest that
all
gaps in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
–spectrum of
\mathcal{H}^{\pm\delta}
are filled with “edge spectrum”. These papers do not provide information on the type of spectrum filling bulk gaps or the nature of the corresponding modes. Results in
[
22
,
42
]
for a discrete variant of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
suggest that the gaps are filled with absolutely continuous spectrum, although there could be other spectral types. An important unanswered question is the following:

Question
.

What are the detailed properties of the states associated with the spectrum that fills the bulk spectral gaps, in particular their character along non-commensurate edges and their decay behavior transverse to the edge?

In this paper and its follow-up
[
4
]
, we address this question.

1.3.
Main contributions

We summarize our main contributions in what follows.

1.3.1.
Definition of edge states for irrational edges

Although
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
is not translation-invariant along irrational edges, we show in Section
3.4
that it extends to an
augmented
Schrödinger operator, which is degenerate elliptic, formally defined for
({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
by

\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}:=-\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot\big(\mskip 1.5mu{\operatorname{Id}}-\delta\,\kappa\big(\delta{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot({\bm{x}}+s{\mathbf{v}}_{2})\big)\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\big)\,\nabla_{\bm{x}}+V({\bm{x}}).

It can be seen from its expression that
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
interpolates, via the domain wall function
\kappa(\cdot)
, between bulk augmented operators
-\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\mskip 1.5mu{\operatorname{Id}}\pm\delta\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}\mskip 1.5mu)\,\nabla_{\bm{x}}+V({\bm{x}})
acting on functions of
({\bm{x}},s)
, across the two-dimensional interface
\Gamma=\left\{({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\ /\ {\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot({\bm{x}}+s{\mathbf{v}}_{2})=0\right\}
.

In Section
3.4.1
, we show that
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
is invariant under the translations

\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{x}}\\
s\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{x}}\\
s\end{pmatrix}+{\mathbf{a}}_{j},\quad j=1,2,\quad\text{where}\quad{\mathbf{a}}_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\\
r\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{a}}_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\\
-1\end{pmatrix},

and where
\Gamma=\vect\{{\mathbf{a}}_{1},{\mathbf{a}}_{2}\}
. Accordingly, in Section
3.4.2
, we define edge states as functions that can be expressed as
\Psi({\bm{x}})=\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s)\big|_{s=0}
, where
\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}
is a nontrivial solution of the eigenvalue problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}\,\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s)&=E\,\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s),\\[8.0pt]
\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{1},s+r)&=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5muk_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}\,\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s),\\[8.0pt]
\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{2},s-1)&=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5muk_{0}}\,\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s),\\[8.0pt]
\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s)\to 0,&|s|\to+\infty.\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

In other words,
\Psi_{\textnormal{aug}}
is pseudo-periodic within the interface
\Gamma
and decays in the direction transverse to
\Gamma
. Without loss of generality, we set
k_{0}=0
. A solution of (
1.1
) is called an
augmented edge state with parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}
. We shall study (
1.1
) in the space

L^{2}_{k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{aug}})=\Big\{F\in L^{2}_{\textit{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\ \ \big|\ \ \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5muk_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}({\mathfrak{K}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{x}})}\;F\in L^{2}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{aug}})\Big\},\quad\text{where}\quad\Sigma_{\textnormal{aug}}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}/(\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{a}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{a}}_{2}).

If (
1.1
) admits a solution that is continuous in
s
, its restriction to
s=0
yields a two-dimensional edge state
\Psi({\bm{x}})
. Such a state is plane wave-like along
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
in the sense that its restriction to
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
is a
quasiperiodic
function; see Remark
3.2
.

Although the above “lifting procedure” restores translation-invariance in three dimensions, the study of (
1.1
) is more delicate, because
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
is a degenerate elliptic operator; it does not involve derivatives with respect to the new variable
s
.

1.3.2.
Multiscale construction of formal augmented edge states

In Section
4
, we formally construct a family
\{\Psi_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{I}}}\}_{{\mathbf{I}}\in\mathbb{L}(\delta)}
of augmented edge states, that is, solutions to (
1.1
), with parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}={\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
, where
\mathbb{L}(\delta)
is a countable subset of
\mathbb{L}:=\{{\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}\}\times\mathbb{Z}
depending on
\delta
and
r
only. For every
{\mathbf{I}}=({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},m)\in\mathbb{L}(\delta)
, the formally constructed solution
\Psi_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{I}}}
bifurcates from the Dirac point, and for
\delta
small, has the structure of a wave packet, whose slowly varying envelope is an eigenfunction of an effective Dirac operator
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}_{\mathbf{I}}

acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})
. This effective operator appears in
[
61
,
111
,
46
,
43
]
; we refer to (
4.13
,
4.14
) for its precise definition.

While the multiscale procedure for constructing edge states was used in previous works for rational edges, a novel aspect of this work, caused by the non-ellipticity of (
1.1
), is the emergence of a countable family of effective Dirac operators, and the implied
countably infinite
family of approximate augmented edge states (with countable index set
\mathbb{L}(\delta)
). For irrational edges, each formal solution
\Psi_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{I}}}
of (
1.1
) restricted to
s=0
yields a distinct,
{\mathbf{I}}
–dependent two-dimensional edge state; see Section
4.4
.

Remark 1.1
.

For rational edges, all the formal solutions of (
1.1
) restricted to
s=0
give rise to only a finite number of distinct two-dimensional edge states.

1.3.3.
Resolvent expansion

For
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}\in[-\pi,\pi]
, solutions to (
1.1
) are eigenfunctions of
\smash{\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}}
, the augmented Schrödinger operator
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
acting on
\smash{L^{2}_{k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{aug}})}
. Since eigenvalues of
\smash{\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}}
are poles of its resolvent, we expect an expansion, for
\delta
small, of the resolvent of
\smash{\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}}
, at energies near
E_{D}
, to involve resolvents of the effective Dirac operators
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}_{\mathbf{I}}
arising from the multiscale expansion. Indeed, in Theorem
7.1
we prove: There exist operators

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\delta}:L^{2}_{k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}={\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{aug}})

\displaystyle\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{L};L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{\delta}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{L};L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2}))

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}={\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{aug}}),

bounded uniformly in
\delta
, such that for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, as
\delta\to 0
,

\bigg(\mskip 1.5mu\frac{\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}={\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}-E_{D}}{\delta}-z\mskip 1.5mu\bigg)^{-1}\ -\ \widehat{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{\delta}\;\big(\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}-z\mskip 1.5mu\big)^{-1}\;\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\delta}\ \to\ 0.

(1.2)

Here,
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
is the block-diagonal Dirac operator

\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}=\begin{pmatrix}\ddots&&(0)\\
&\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}_{\mathbf{I}}&\\
(0)&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}_{{\mathbf{I}}\in\mathbb{L}}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{L};L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2}))\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{L};L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})).

In the case of rational edges, analogous resolvent expansions were established in
[
46
,
43
]
for the two-dimensional Schrödinger operator,
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
, acting on a suitable space. Equation (
1.2
), though formulated in terms of a three-dimensional operator, provides a sense in which these resolvent expansions generalize to the case of irrational edges. In fact, Theorem
A.2
shows that (
1.2
) is consistent with the results in
[
46
,
43
]
when
r
is rational.

A key assumption under which we derive (
1.2
) is an
omnidirectional no-fold
condition, which we introduce and motivate in Section
6.2
. The validity of this condition in the strong binding regime has been established in
[
60
]
.

We remark on applications of the resolvent expansion in Theorem
7.1
. It can be combined with the Helffer-Sjöstrand formula to estimate operators of the form
w(\mskip 1.5mu\delta^{-1}\,(\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}-E_{D})\mskip 1.5mu)
, with
w\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
; see Corollary
B.1
. In particular, with appropriate choices of
w
, Corollary
B.1
enables us to estimate smoothed spectral projections of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}
near
E_{D}
, or to derive large time effective dynamics of
\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,t\,\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}})
, for wave packet initial conditions, which are spectrally located near the Dirac point.

1.4.
Open questions and future directions: From the eigenpairs of
\mathcal{D}^{\delta}
to genuine augmented edge states

The spectrum of the block-diagonal effective Hamiltonian,
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
, decomposes into an absolutely continuous part
\mathbb{R}\setminus(-\theta_{\textnormal{gap}},\theta_{\textnormal{gap}})
and a pure point part. When
r
is irrational, the point spectrum of
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
is dense, with some of its eigenvalues that densely fill the interval
(-\theta_{\textnormal{gap}},\theta_{\textnormal{gap}})
, while its other eigenvalues are embedded in the absolutely continuous spectrum; see Figure
7
. Two questions arise from the present article:

•

Do the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
in
(-\theta_{\textnormal{gap}},\theta_{\textnormal{gap}})
seed genuine eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}
?

•

How do the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
embedded in its absolutely continuous spectrum manifest in the spectral properties of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}
?

What makes these questions challenging is that the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
, embedded or not, are not “
well-separated
’’
1
1

1

By “well-separated” eigenvalue
z
, we mean that there is an interval
z+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
that does not contain any other eigenvalue of
\mathcal{D}^{\mskip 1.5mu\delta}
. This applies to isolated eigenvalues, but even eigenvalues embedded in the continuous spectrum can be well-separated in that sense.
from one another, and therefore general arguments (based on quasi-modes) for gapped self-adjoint operators
[
46
,
43
,
40
]
do not apply. In fact, regarding the first question, in perturbation theory of general linear operators, it is well-known that dense point spectrum is unstable under general perturbations; see
[
39
,
27
,
138
,
92
]
. Proving that the dense eigenvalues of
\mathcal{D}^{\delta}
seed eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}
is therefore a delicate task.

Concerning the first question, we shall provide an affirmative answer in
[
4
]
.
Specifically, we prove that if the irrational edge parameter,
r
, satisfies a generic
Diophantine
condition, then the operator
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}
admits a
dense
set of eigenvalues near the Dirac energy
E_{D}
. Further, the associated eigenfunctions are continuous in
s
, and their restrictions to
s=0
define genuine two-dimensional edge states. This result indicates that the energies populating the bulk
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
–gap of the two-dimensional edge operator
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
correspond to states which are quasiperiodic along the edge, and decaying in the direction transverse to the edge.

As for the second question, while “well-separated” eigenvalues embedded in continuous spectrum are known under certain conditions to perturb to scattering resonances
[
68
,
91
,
74
,
143
,
33
]
(see also
[
132
, Section XII.6]
and
[
90
, Chapter 16]
), it is not clear how an embedded dense set of eigenvalues of
\mathcal{D}^{\delta}
behaves under perturbation.

1.5.
Outline

In Section
2
, we review Floquet-Bloch theory for two-dimensional Schrödinger operators with periodic potentials, introduce the notion of honeycomb lattice potentials, and recall existing results on the existence of Dirac points of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. In Section
3
, we present the construction of the edge operator,
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
, for an arbitrary edge orientation,
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Section
3.1
introduces the bulk perturbed operators, and Section
3.2
describes their interpolation across an edge. The definition of edge states for rational edges is recalled in Section
3.3
. In Section
3.4
, we propose a notion of edge state for irrational edges: using the notion of quasiperiodicity, these states are defined as restrictions of three-dimensional augmented edge states. In Section
4
, we describe the multiscale asymptotic expansion procedure used to formally construct augmented edge states. These edge states are seeded by the point eigenvalues of emergent effective Dirac operators. The spectral properties of these effective Dirac operators are also discussed; see Section
4.3
.
In Section
5.1
, we introduce an appropriate Sobolev space framework for the rigorous study of the augmented edge state eigenvalue problem. We then show in Section
5.2
that a useful tool for the analysis is the Fourier transform with respect to the augmented variable,
s
. Section
6
introduces the notion of edge quasi-momentum slices, parameterizing those Floquet-Bloch modes which participate in the construction of augmented edge states. We also present and motivate the omnidirectional no-fold condition, and highlight its link with the
directional
no-fold condition used in previous work for rational edges. The omnidirectional no-fold condition facilitates spectral localization of the Hamiltonian to a small neighborhood of the Dirac point, allowing for the proof of
(i)
the resolvent expansion of Theorem
7.1
, and
(ii)
the existence of quasiperiodic edge states of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
.
Section
7
contains the precise statement for the resolvent expansion (Theorem
7.1
) along with some related discussion. In Sections
8
and
9
, we present the proof of Theorem
7.1
using a Schur complement reduction strategy. Appendix
A
shows how the resolvent expansion in Theorem
7.1
reduces to known results for rational edges; see Theorem
A.2
.
Appendix
B
presents an application of the resolvent expansion in Theorem
7.1
by expanding smooth functions of the centered and rescaled operator
\delta^{-1}\,(\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\textnormal{aug}},{\mathbf{K}}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}-E_{D})
; see Corollary
B.1
.

Notations and conventions

•

\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\{0,1,2,...\}
and
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,...\}
denote the sets of non-negative and positive integers, respectively.

•

Given
d\geq 1
and
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d}),{\bm{y}}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{y}}:=x_{1}y_{1}+\dots+x_{d}y_{d}
and
|{\bm{x}}|^{2}:={\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{x}}
. For
d=2
, we define
{\bm{x}}\wedge{\bm{y}}=x_{1}y_{2}-x_{2}y_{1}
.

•

Given two Hilbert spaces
(\mathscr{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{X}})
,
(\mathscr{Y},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{Y}})
, we call
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
the space of
bounded
linear operators from
\mathscr{X}
to
\mathscr{Y}
, with the norm
\smash{\displaystyle\|T\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})}:=\!\!\!\!\!\sup_{x\in\mathscr{X},\|x\|_{\mathscr{X}}=1}\!\!\!\|T\,x\|_{\mathscr{Y}}}
for any
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
. We set
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}):=\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{X})
.

•

Given
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{X}})
, we call
L^{2}(S;\mathscr{X})
the space of
\mathscr{X}
–valued square-integrable functions, that is,
{\displaystyle L^{2}(S;\mathscr{X}):=\Big\{u\ \Big|\ \int_{S}\|u(t)\|^{2}_{\mathscr{X}}\,dt<\infty\Big\}}
. This is a Hilbert space, equipped with the natural norm arising from its definition. Similarly, given
\mathbb{I}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{I};\mathscr{X})
as the space of
\mathscr{X}
–valued square-integrable sequences, that is,
\displaystyle\ell^{2}(\mathbb{I};\mathscr{X}):=\Big\{(g_{\mathbf{I}})_{{\mathbf{I}}\in\mathbb{I}}\in\mathscr{X}^{\mathbb{I}}\ \Big|\ \sum_{{\mathbf{I}}\in\mathbb{I}}\|g_{\mathbf{I}}\|^{2}_{\mathscr{X}}<\infty\Big\}
.

•

Given
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
a\lesssim b
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
a\leq C\mskip 1.5mub
. We write
a\simeq b
if
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
.

•

If
u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathscr{X}
and
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
, we write
u_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}(f(\varepsilon))
if there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0},C>0
such that
\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C\,f(\varepsilon)
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
.

•

Given a Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\|\cdot\|_{{}_{\mathscr{X}}})
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{X}
, we denote by
\varphi\otimes\varphi
the rank-one operator defined by
(\varphi\otimes\varphi)\,u:=\langle\varphi,\,u\rangle_{{}_{\mathscr{X}}}\,\varphi
for any
u\in\mathscr{X}
. Note that
\varphi\otimes\varphi
is an orthogonal projector if
\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{X}}=1
.

•

The domain of an unbounded operator
\mathcal{H}
is
\textnormal{D\kern 0.83344pt}(\mathcal{H})
, and its spectrum is
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{H})
.

•

Following the convention
[
79
]
,
\operatorname{frac}\left(x\right)=x-\lfloor x\rfloor\in[0,1)
is the fractional part of
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Here,
\lfloor x\rfloor\in(x-1,x]
denotes the greatest integer less than or equal to
x
.

•

\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
and
\Lambda^{*}:=\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
respectively denote the equilateral triangular lattice and its dual, with

{\mathbf{v}}_{1}:=\varsigma\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\sqrt{3}/2\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle 1/2\end{pmatrix},\ {\mathbf{v}}_{2}:=\varsigma\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\sqrt{3}/2\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle-1/2\end{pmatrix},\ \ {\mathbf{k}}_{1}:=\varsigma\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle 1/2\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle\sqrt{3}/2\end{pmatrix},\ {\mathbf{k}}_{2}:=\varsigma\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle 1/2\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle-\sqrt{3}/2\end{pmatrix},

(1.3)

and where
\varsigma^{2}=2/\sqrt{3}
is such that
|{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\wedge{\mathbf{v}}_{2}|=1
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{n}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{l}=\delta_{nl}
for any
n,l\in\{1,2\}
.

•

{\mathbf{K}}:=2\pi\,({\mathbf{k}}_{1}-{\mathbf{k}}_{2})/3
and
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}:=-{\mathbf{K}}=2\pi\,(-{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2})/3
are high-symmetry quasi-momenta, which generate the union of dual sublattices
\mathbb{K}:=({\mathbf{K}}+\Lambda^{*})\cup({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}+\Lambda^{*})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

•

A generic element of
\mathbb{L}:=\{{\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}\}\times\mathbb{Z}
is denoted
{\mathbf{I}}=({\mathbf{K}}_{\mathbf{I}},m_{\mathbf{I}})
. We often refer to subsets of
\mathbb{L}
as
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{L}
.

•

The Pauli matrices are

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{3}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(1.4)

•

We consider the unitary Fourier transform and its inverse given for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by

\mathcal{F}f({\lambda}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\zeta)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\lambda}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta}\,d\zeta,\quad(\mathcal{F}^{*}g)(\zeta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g({\lambda})\,\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\lambda}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta}\,d{\lambda}.

(1.5)

We recall the Plancherel identity
\|\mathcal{F}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
.

•

Fourier multiplier,
h(D_{\zeta})
: Let
h\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Define

D_{\zeta}:=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{\zeta},

(1.6)

and, using (
1.5
), the bounded linear operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

h(D_{\zeta})f(\zeta)=(\mathcal{F}^{*}\,h\,\mathcal{F})f(\zeta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h({\lambda})\,\widehat{f}({\lambda})\,\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\lambda}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta}\,d{\lambda},\quad\text{where}\quad\widehat{f}({\lambda}):=\mathcal{F}\,f({\lambda}).

(1.7)

•

Let
{\lambda}\mapsto\chi({\lambda})
denote the indicator function of the interval
[-1,1]
. We shall frequently spectrally localize on a set of size
\delta^{\nu}
using the operator
\chi(\delta^{-\nu}D_{\zeta})
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.
Floquet-Bloch theory, honeycomb lattice potentials, and Dirac points

We first review Floquet-Bloch theory; see,
e.g.

[
105
,
108
,
107
,
109
,
71
]
or
[
132
, Chapter XIII]
.

2.1.
Fourier analysis in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)

Let
\{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\}
be a linearly independent set in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We introduce:

•

the
lattice

\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
;

•

the
fundamental unit cell

\Omega:=\left\{\mathrm{y}_{1}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathrm{y}_{2}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\ /\ \mathrm{y}_{1},\mathrm{y}_{2}\in(0,1)\right\}
;

•

the
dual lattice

\Lambda^{*}:=\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
, where
{\mathbf{k}}_{n}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{l}=\delta_{nl}
for
n,l\in\{1,2\}
;

•

the
first Brillouin zone

\mathscr{B}
, a fundamental unit cell defined as the set of points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that are closer to the origin than to any other point in the dual lattice
\Lambda^{*}
.

Given
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}
be the space of
{\mathbf{k}}
–pseudo-periodic functions with respect to
\Lambda
:

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}:=\big\{\mskip 1.5muf\in L^{2}_{\textit{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ \ \big|\ \ f({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}})=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}}\,f({\bm{x}})\quad\text{for}\quad{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},{\mathbf{v}}\in\Lambda\mskip 1.5mu\big\}.

(2.1)

Note that
f
is
{\mathbf{k}}
–pseudo-periodic with respect to
\Lambda
if and only if
\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\,f
is
\Lambda
–periodic. In particular,
L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)
for
{\mathbf{k}}=0
. We equip
L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}
with the inner product and the norm

\langle g,f\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}}:=\int_{\Omega}\overline{g({\bm{x}})}\,f({\bm{x}})\,d{\bm{x}},\quad\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}}:=\langle f,f\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}}.

Similarly, one defines the Sobolev space

\forall\;q\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\quad H^{q}_{\mathbf{k}}:=\big\{\mskip 1.5muf\in L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}\ \ \big|\ \ \mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}f\in H^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)\mskip 1.5mu\big\},

equipped with the
H^{q}(\Omega)
–norm. Note that
H^{q=0}_{\mathbf{k}}=L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}
.

Any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)
can be expanded in Fourier series:

f({\bm{x}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in\Lambda^{*}}\widehat{f}_{\mathbf{k}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}},\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{f}_{\mathbf{k}}:=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{y}}}\,f({\bm{y}})\,d{\bm{y}}.

Moreover, the Sobolev regularity of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)
is related to the decay rate of its Fourier coefficients
[
145
, Chapter 4.3]
:

\forall\;q\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\quad\forall\;f\in H^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda),\quad\|f\|^{2}_{H^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)}\hyperref@@ii[item:notation_lesssim_simeq]{\simeq}\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in\Lambda^{*}}(1+|{\mathbf{k}}|^{2})^{q}\,|\widehat{f}_{\mathbf{k}}|^{2}.

(2.2)

2.2.
Floquet-Bloch states

Given
V\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda;\mathbb{R})
, a real-valued, smooth, and
\Lambda
–periodic potential, we consider the self-adjoint Schrödinger operator:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{0}

\displaystyle:=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle\textnormal{D\kern 0.83344pt}(\mathcal{H}^{0})

\displaystyle:=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

In addition, given
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle:=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle\textnormal{D\kern 0.83344pt}(\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{k}})

\displaystyle:=H^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}.

The spectral properties of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
can be studied using the eigenpairs of
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{k}}
, for
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which correspond to the nontrivial solutions of the eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{k}}\,\Phi({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})&E({\mathbf{k}})\,\Phi({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}}),&{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\[8.0pt]
\Phi({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}};{\mathbf{k}})&\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}}\,\Phi({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}}),&{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\ \ \forall\;{\mathbf{v}}\in\Lambda.\end{array}\right.

(2.3)

The
{\mathbf{k}}
–pseudo-periodicity condition in (
2.3
) is invariant under the translation
{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto{\mathbf{k}}+{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}
for any
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{*}
, so that the study of (
2.3
) can be restricted to
{\mathbf{k}}
varying in the Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
. A solution of (
2.3
) is called a
Floquet-Bloch eigenpair
with quasi-momentum
{\mathbf{k}}
.

Expressing Floquet-Bloch eigenfunctions in the form
\Phi({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\,p({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})
, the eigenvalue problem (
2.3
) becomes:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathcal{H}^{0}({\mathbf{k}})\,p({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})&E({\mathbf{k}})\,p({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}}),&{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\[8.0pt]
p({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}};{\mathbf{k}})&p({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}}),&{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\ \ \forall\;{\mathbf{v}}\in\Lambda,\end{array}\right.

(2.4)

where

\mathcal{H}^{0}({\mathbf{k}}):=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu-\mathrm{i}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\;\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{k}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}=-(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\,{\mathbf{k}})^{2}+V({\bm{x}}),\qquad\textnormal{D\kern 0.83344pt}(\mathcal{H}^{0}({\mathbf{k}})):=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda).

For each fixed
{\mathbf{k}}
,
\mathcal{H}^{0}({\mathbf{k}})
(equivalently,
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{k}}
) is a self-adjoint operator with compact resolvent, and hence its spectrum is a countable set of real eigenvalues, listed with multiplicity:

\displaystyle E_{1}({\mathbf{k}})\leq E_{2}({\mathbf{k}})\leq E_{3}({\mathbf{k}})\leq\dots\leq E_{b}({\mathbf{k}})\leq\dots;\quad\lim_{b\to+\infty}E_{b}({\mathbf{k}})=+\infty,

with eigenfunctions
p_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})
,
b\geq 1
. For a fixed
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathscr{B}
,
\{{\bm{x}}\mapsto p_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\}_{b\geq 1}
can be taken to be an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda)
. With this choice,
\{{\bm{x}}\mapsto\Phi_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}}):=\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\,p_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\}_{b\geq 1}
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}
. By elliptic regularity, the smoothness of
V({\bm{x}})
implies that the functions
{\bm{x}}\mapsto p_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})
and
{\bm{x}}\mapsto\Phi_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})
are smooth, uniformly in
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathscr{B}
. Moreover, similarly to (
2.2
),

\forall\;({\mathbf{k}},q)\in\mathscr{B}\times\mathbb{N}_{0},\quad\forall\;f\in H^{q}_{\mathbf{k}},\quad\|f\|^{2}_{H^{q}_{\mathbf{k}}}\hyperref@@ii[item:notation_lesssim_simeq]{\simeq}\sum_{b\geq 1}(1+b^{2})^{q/2}\,\big|\left\langle\mskip 1.5mu\Phi_{b}(\cdot\,;{\mathbf{k}}),f\mskip 1.5mu\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}}\big|^{2},

(2.5)

where the implied constants can be chosen to be independent in
{\mathbf{k}}
.

The sets
\left\{({\mathbf{k}},E_{b}({\mathbf{k}})),\ {\mathbf{k}}\in\mathscr{B}\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}\times\mathbb{R}
, for
b\geq 1
, are called
dispersion surfaces
, and the mappings
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathscr{B}\mapsto E_{b}({\mathbf{k}})
,
dispersion functions
. These functions are Lipschitz continuous; see
[
105
,
9
,
108
]
and
[
63
, Appendix A]
. For
b\geq 1
, as
{\mathbf{k}}
varies over
\mathscr{B}
, each function
E_{b}({\mathbf{k}})
ranges over a closed interval called the
b
–th band
. The union of these bands over
b\geq 1
forms the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
–spectrum of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. Moreover,
\{{\bm{x}}\mapsto\Phi_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\}_{b\geq 1,{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathscr{B}}
is complete in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

\displaystyle\forall\;f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad f({\bm{x}})=\sum_{b\geq 1}\int_{\mathscr{B}}\left\langle\mskip 1.5mu\Phi_{b}(\cdot\,;{\mathbf{k}}),f\mskip 1.5mu\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\;\Phi_{b}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\,d\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}},\quad{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

2.3.
Equilateral triangular lattice

We now apply the results of Section
2.2
to the equilateral triangular lattice
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
and its dual
\Lambda^{*}:=\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
, where
({\mathbf{v}}_{1},{\mathbf{v}}_{2},{\mathbf{k}}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
are defined by (
1.3
) and shown in Figure
2
. The Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
is a closed regular hexagon shown in Figure
2
(right panel). Consider its vertices
{\mathbf{K}}
and
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
defined by

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}:=\frac{2\pi}{3}({\mathbf{k}}_{1}-{\mathbf{k}}_{2}),\quad{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}:=-{\mathbf{K}}=\frac{2\pi}{3}(-{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2}).

The vertices of
\mathscr{B}
can be generated from
{\mathbf{K}}
,
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
using the clockwise rotation matrix by
2\pi/3
:

\displaystyle R=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle-1/2&\displaystyle\sqrt{3}/2\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle-\sqrt{3}/2&\displaystyle-1/2\end{pmatrix}.

(2.6)

These vertices are classified into two categories:

•

{\mathbf{K}}
type-points:
{\mathbf{K}}
,
R\,{\mathbf{K}}={\mathbf{K}}+2\pi\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{2}
, and
R^{2}\,{\mathbf{K}}={\mathbf{K}}-2\pi\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{1}
;

•

{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
type-points:
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
,
R\,{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}-2\pi\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{2}
, and
R^{2}\,{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}+2\pi\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{1}
.

The union of sublattices generated by the vertices of
\mathscr{B}
is denoted
\mathbb{K}:=({\mathbf{K}}+\Lambda^{*})\cup({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}+\Lambda^{*})
.

Figure 2
.
Left: The equilateral triangular lattice
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
and the periodicity cell
\Omega
.
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the union of lattice translates of
\Omega
and within this cell, are two lattice points. The honeycomb lattice is the union of the two triangular lattices, based at each of these points. Right: The dual lattice
\Lambda^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
and the Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
, whose vertices are the high-symmetry quasi-momenta of
{\mathbf{K}}
and
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
–type.

2.4.
Honeycomb lattice potentials

Consider the three symmetry operators: complex-conjugation, inversion and rotation by
2\pi/3
:

\\
(\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{C}f\mskip 1.5mu)\mskip 1.5mu({\bm{x}}):=\overline{f({\bm{x}})},\quad(\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{P}f\mskip 1.5mu)\mskip 1.5mu({\bm{x}}):=f(-{\bm{x}}),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}f({\bm{x}}):=f(R^{*}{\bm{x}}),

where
R
is given by (
2.6
), and
R^{*}=R^{\top}=R^{-1}
.

Definition 2.1

(Definition 2.1,
[
62
]
)
.

A function
V\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is said to be a
honeycomb lattice potential
if
V
is real-valued,
\Lambda
–periodic and, with respect to some origin of coordinates which we may take to be
{\bm{x}}=0
,
V({\bm{x}})
is also even, and
2\pi/3
–rotationally invariant:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{C},V({\bm{x}})]=0,\quad[\mathcal{P},V({\bm{x}})]=0,\quad\text{and}\quad[\mathcal{R},V({\bm{x}})]=0.

Due to the
\mathcal{R}
–invariance of a honeycomb lattice potential
V
, the operator
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}})
, acting on
L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}
, commutes with
\mathcal{R}
if and only if
{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}
is a vertex of the Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
modulo translations
\Lambda^{*}
; see
[
62
, Proposition 2.2]
. For this reason, the vertices of
\mathscr{B}
are called
high-symmetry quasi-momenta
. Moreover, the
L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}
–spectrum of
\mathcal{R}
consists of the cube roots of unity
1
,
\tau:=\mathrm{e}^{2\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\pi/3}
, and
\overline{\tau}
, and hence
L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}
can be decomposed in terms of the eigenspaces of
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},1}\oplus L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},\tau}\oplus L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},\overline{\tau}},

where

\displaystyle L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},z}

\displaystyle:=\big\{\mskip 1.5muf\in L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}\ /\ \ \mathcal{R}f=zf\mskip 1.5mu\big\},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}.

2.5.
Dirac points

A
Dirac point
is a quasi-momentum/energy pair
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
for which the following holds: there exist an integer
b_{\star}\geq 1
and Floquet-Bloch eigenpair mappings

\displaystyle{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto\big(E_{b_{\star}}({\mathbf{k}}),\Phi_{b_{\star}}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto\big(E_{b_{\star}+1}({\mathbf{k}}),\Phi_{b_{\star}+1}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\big),

such that the dispersion functions
E_{-}:{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto E_{b_{\star}}({\mathbf{k}})
and
E_{+}:{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto E_{b_{\star}+1}({\mathbf{k}})
touch conically at
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
. More precisely, there exist
\upsilon_{D}>0
, constants
C,\;\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and functions
e_{\pm}({\mathbf{k}})
, defined for
|{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}|<\varepsilon_{0}
with
|e_{\pm}({\mathbf{k}})|\leq C|{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}|
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}E_{-}({\mathbf{k}})-E_{D}&-\upsilon_{D}\,|{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}|\,(1+e_{-}({\mathbf{k}})),\\[8.0pt]
E_{+}({\mathbf{k}})-E_{D}&+\upsilon_{D}\,|{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}|\,(1+e_{+}({\mathbf{k}})).\end{array}

(2.7)

The number
\upsilon_{D}
is called the
Dirac velocity
.

The following result provides sufficient conditions for the existence of Dirac points at the vertices of the Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
; see
[
62
]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}
be a vertex of the Brillouin zone
\mathscr{B}
, and let
E_{D}\in\mathbb{R}
.

A sufficient condition for the quasi-momentum/energy pair
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
to be a
Dirac point
of
\mathcal{H}^{0}=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}})
is the existence of an integer
b_{\star}\geq 1
and Floquet-Bloch eigenpairs mappings

\displaystyle{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto\big(E_{b_{\star}}({\mathbf{k}}),\Phi_{b_{\star}}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{k}}\mapsto\big(E_{b_{\star}+1}({\mathbf{k}}),\Phi_{b_{\star}+1}({\bm{x}};{\mathbf{k}})\big),

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i).

E_{D}=E_{b_{\star}}({\mathbf{K}}_{\star})=E_{b_{\star}+1}({\mathbf{K}}_{\star})
is a double eigenvalue of
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}
;

(ii).

There is a

Dirac eigenbasis
, consisting of functions

\displaystyle\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1}\in L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},\tau}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}=\mathcal{P}\,\mathcal{C}\,\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1}\in L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},\overline{\tau}},

with
\langle\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{j},\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{l}\rangle_{L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}}=\delta_{jl}
for
j,l\in\{1,2\}
, and

\Ker\,(\mathcal{H}^{0}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}-E_{D})=\vect\big\{\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1},\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}\big\};

(iii).

One has

\displaystyle\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}:=\frac{1}{2}\,\overline{\big\langle\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1},-2\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\nabla\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}\big\rangle_{L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}\neq 0.

(2.8)

If the above conditions are satisfied, then (
2.7
) holds with
\upsilon_{D}:=|\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}|
. Moreover, if (
2.8
) holds, then there exists
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
such that by replacing
\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1}
with
\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\,\theta}\,\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1}
, and
\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}
with
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\,\theta}\,\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}
, we obtain an equivalent Dirac basis that satisfies Condition

(

⁢
i
i

)

, and such that
\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}\equiv\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}(\theta)
is a real number, with
\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}}=\upsilon_{D}>0
and
\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}}=-\upsilon_{D}<0
.

Remark 2.3
.

(a).

Consider a honeycomb lattice potential
V
such that
\mathcal{H}^{0}=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}})
admits a Dirac point
({\mathbf{K}},E_{D})
. Since
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathbf{K}}=\mathcal{H}^{0}_{R{\mathbf{K}}}=\mathcal{H}^{0}_{R^{2}{\mathbf{K}}}
, it follows that
(R{\mathbf{K}},E_{D})
and
(R^{2}{\mathbf{K}},E_{D})
are also Dirac points.

(b).

Let
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
be a Dirac point with eigenbasis
\{\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1},\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}\}
in the sense of Proposition
2.2
. Since
V
is even,
(-{\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
is also a Dirac point with associated eigenbasis
\{\mathcal{P}\,\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1},\mathcal{P}\,\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{2}\}
.

The existence of Dirac points is shown in
[
62
]
for generic honeycomb lattice potentials.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
V
be a honeycomb lattice potential (see Definition
2.1
) such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}V({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu2\pi\mskip 1.5mu({\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2})\cdot{\bm{x}}}\;d{\bm{x}}\neq 0.

Then there exists a discrete set
S\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}\setminus S
, the operator
-\Delta+\mu\mskip 1.5muV({\bm{x}})
satisfies the conditions in Proposition
2.2
. As a consequence,
-\Delta+\mu\mskip 1.5muV({\bm{x}})
admits a Dirac point
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
for
{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}\in\{{\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}\}
.

Following Theorem
2.4
, we henceforth assume that
V
is a honeycomb lattice potential in the sense of Definition
2.1
, such that
\mathcal{H}^{0}=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}})
has a Dirac point
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})\in\{{\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}\}\times\mathbb{R}
, and satisfies the conditions in Proposition
2.2
. Moreover, following the last part of Proposition
2.2
, we always choose the Dirac eigenbasis so that
\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}}=-\upsilon^{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}}=\upsilon_{D}>0
.

3.
Honeycomb structures with a line defect

We are interested in a class of operators introduced in
[
61
,
111
,
43
]
as a model of a honeycomb structure with a line defect or
edge
. The starting point is the unperturbed operator
\mathcal{H}^{0}=-\Delta+V({\bm{x}})
, where
V
is a honeycomb lattice potential in the sense of Definition
2.1
. In light of Theorem
2.4
and Remark
2.3.

(
a
)

, we assume that
\mathcal{H}^{0}
admits a Dirac point
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
with
{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}\in\{{\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}\}
.

3.1.
Conjugation-breaking perturbations

Let
a:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. We assume that

(
A
.1)

a\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is smooth and
\Lambda
–periodic
;

(
A
.2)

a
is
real-valued
and
even
,
i.e.

[\mathcal{C},a({\bm{x}})]=0
and
[\mathcal{P},a({\bm{x}})]=0
.

We then define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\delta}:=-\nabla\cdot\big(\mskip 1.5mu{\operatorname{Id}}-\delta\,a({\bm{x}})\,\hyperref@@ii[eq:def_pauli_matrices]{\sigma_{2}}\mskip 1.5mu\big)\,\nabla+V({\bm{x}}),\qquad\textnormal{D\kern 0.83344pt}(\mathcal{H}^{\delta}):=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Since
\mathcal{P}\,a\,\sigma_{2}=a\,\sigma_{2}\,\mathcal{P}
but
\mathcal{C}\,a\,\sigma_{2}=-a\,\sigma_{2}\,\mathcal{C}
, the perturbation induced by
a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}
preserves parity symmetry, while conjugation symmetry is broken:
[\mathcal{H}^{\delta},\mathcal{P}]=0
and
[\mathcal{H}^{\delta},\mathcal{C}]\neq 0
. Moreover, because
\mathcal{C}\,\mathcal{H}^{\delta}=\mathcal{H}^{-\delta}\,\mathcal{C}
, the operators
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}
and
\mathcal{H}^{-\delta}
share the same spectrum.

In what follows, we shall also assume that

(
A
.3)

the
non-degeneracy condition
:

\vartheta^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}:=\left\langle\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1},\nabla\cdot\big(a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}\,\nabla\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1}\big)\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}}\neq 0

is satisfied, where
\Phi^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}_{1}
is an element of the Dirac eigenbasis associated to
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
; see Condition

(

⁢
i
i

)

in Proposition
2.2
.

Under (

A

.1
)–(

A

.3
), for a small enough
\delta\neq 0
, the
\mathcal{C}
-breaking perturbation opens a
local gap
in the neighborhood of the Dirac point
({\mathbf{K}}_{\star},E_{D})
. More precisely, for all
{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathscr{B}
define:

\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\mathbf{k}}:=-\nabla\cdot\big(\mskip 1.5mu{\operatorname{Id}}-\delta\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\big)\,\nabla+V({\bm{x}}),\qquad\textnormal{D\kern 0.83344pt}(\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\mathbf{k}}):=H^{2}_{\mathbf{k}}\ .

Then, there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0}>0
such that for any
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
and
|{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,

\operatorname{Spec}\big(\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{{\mathbf{k}}}\big)\cap(c^{\delta}_{-},c^{\delta}_{+})=\emptyset,\quad\text{where}\quad c^{\delta}_{\pm}:=E_{D}\pm\delta\,|\vartheta^{{\mathbf{K}}_{\star}}|+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}),

and similarly for
\mathcal{H}^{-\delta}_{\mathbf{k}}
.

3.2.
Domain wall-modulated Schrödinger operator

We construct a class of operators that spatially interpolate smoothly and slowly between
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}
and
\mathcal{H}^{-\delta}
in the direction transverse to an edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
, with
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. The interpolation is realized via a
domain wall function
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
domain wall function
is a function
\kappa\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\kappa^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\to\pm\infty}\kappa(\zeta)=\pm 1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=0
. Given a domain wall function
\kappa
, in the sense of Definition
3.1
, we consider the Schrödinger operator formally defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}:=-\nabla\cdot A^{\delta}({\bm{x}})\,\nabla+V({\bm{x}}),\quad\text{with}\quad A^{\delta}({\bm{x}}):={\operatorname{Id}}-\delta\,\kappa(\delta{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}})\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}.

(3.1)

Given the behavior of
\kappa
at infinity,
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}\,u({\bm{x}})\to\mathcal{H}^{\pm\delta}\,u({\bm{x}})
as
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}}\to\pm\infty
. In other words, far from the edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
,
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
asymptotically behaves as
\mathcal{H}^{\pm\delta}
. We refer to Figure
1
for a schematic representation of
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
.

The edge vector
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
can be written as
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+b_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
where
a_{1},b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}\neq 0
,
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
cannot both vanish, and because the roles of
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
are interchangeable, we can assume, without any loss of generality, that
a_{1}\neq 0
.

As we are interested in states localized near
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
, we give particular attention to the properties of the coefficients
(A^{\delta},V)
in the
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
–direction. In the next sections, we shall highlight the periodic or quasiperiodic nature of
(A^{\delta},V)
along
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+b_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, depending on whether or not the slope
b_{1}/a_{1}
is rational.

3.3.
Translation-invariance along rational edges

We review the setting introduced in
[
61
,
111
,
43
]
for line defects. If
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+b_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
is such that
b_{1}/a_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}
, then we call
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
a
rational edge
. For rational edges,
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
can always be normalized so that
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
are coprime integers. In this case,
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
is an element of
\Lambda
, the triangular lattice. Furthermore, by Bézout’s theorem, there is a pair of coprime integers
(a_{2},b_{2})
such that
a_{1}\mskip 1.5mub_{2}-a_{2}\mskip 1.5mub_{1}=1
. Define
{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}:=a_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+b_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
. Then,
\mathbb{Z}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}=\Lambda
.

To
({\mathfrak{v}}_{1},{\mathfrak{v}}_{2})
, we associate the dual lattice vectors

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}:=b_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{1}-a_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{2},\quad{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}:=-b_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{k}}_{2}\quad(\textnormal{with}\quad 2\pi\,{\mathfrak{K}}_{j}\in\Lambda^{*}),

which satisfy
{\mathfrak{K}}_{n}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}=\delta_{nl}
for
n,l\in\{1,2\}
. One has
\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathfrak{K}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathfrak{K}}_{2})=\Lambda^{*}
.

Classical examples of rational edges include

•

the
zigzag
edge:
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}={\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, and
{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}={\mathbf{k}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}={\mathbf{k}}_{2}
;

•

the
armchair
edge:
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}+{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
,
{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}={\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, and
{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}={\mathbf{k}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}=-{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2}
.

Since
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}\in\Lambda
, the coefficients
(a,V)
, involved in the definition of the bulk operators
\mathcal{H}^{\pm\delta}
, are
\mathbb{Z}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
–periodic. In addition, the relation
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=0
implies that the domain wall-modulated operator
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
defined by (
3.1
) is translation-invariant along
\mathbb{Z}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
:

\displaystyle V({\bm{x}}+{\mathfrak{v}}_{1})=V({\bm{x}})\quad\text{and}\quad A^{\delta}({\bm{x}}+{\mathfrak{v}}_{1})=A^{\delta}({\bm{x}}),\quad{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(3.2)

Related to this periodicity property is a parallel quasi-momentum, denoted
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}
. We may then consider nontrivial solutions, for
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}\in[-\pi,\pi)
, of the eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}\,\Psi({\bm{x}})&E\,\Psi({\bm{x}}),&{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\[8.0pt]
\Psi({\bm{x}}+{\mathfrak{v}}_{1})&\mathrm{e}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5muk_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}}\,\Psi({\bm{x}}),&{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\ \,\Psi({\bm{x}})\to 0,\quad|{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}}|\to+\infty.\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(3.3)

A solution of (
3.3
) is called an
edge state
with parallel quasi-momentum
k_{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\parallel$}}}{\mathrel{\mkern 1.0mu\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\parallel$}}}}
.

3.4.
Irrational edges

We now consider an edge vector
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{1}+b_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
with no a priori assumption on the slope
b_{1}/a_{1}
. By assumption,
a_{1}
is non-zero, so we may normalize
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
by choosing
a_{1}=1
, and set

{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}:={\mathbf{v}}_{1}+r{\mathbf{v}}_{2},\quad\textrm{with}\quad r\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.4)

If
r\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, then we call
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
an
irrational edge
. Note that
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
reduces to two of the classical rational edges: the zigzag (
r=0
) and the armchair (
r=1
)
[
61
]
.

For an irrational slope
r
, the edge vector
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
no longer belongs to the triangular lattice
\Lambda
. Still, we may define
{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}
,
{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}
, and
{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}
similarly to the rational case (Section
3.3
):

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}:={\mathbf{v}}_{1}+r{\mathbf{v}}_{2},\quad{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}:={\mathbf{v}}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}:={\mathbf{k}}_{1},\quad{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}:=-r{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2}.

(3.5)

These vectors satisfy

{\mathfrak{K}}_{n}\cdot{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}=\delta_{nl},\quad\textrm{for}\quad n,l=1,2.

However, note that
\mathbb{Z}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}\neq\Lambda
and
\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathfrak{K}}_{1})+\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu(2\pi{\mathfrak{K}}_{2})\neq\Lambda^{*}
.

3.4.1.
Quasiperiodicity along general edges

We assume that
({\mathfrak{v}}_{1},{\mathfrak{v}}_{2},{\mathfrak{K}}_{1},{\mathfrak{K}}_{2})
are given by (
3.5
) with a slope
r\in\mathbb{R}
. If
r
is irrational, then the coefficients
(A^{\delta},V)
of the domain wall-modulated potential
\mathcal{H}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{dw}}
introduced in (
3.1
) are not periodic in the
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
–direction. However, in the spirit of
[
72
,
20
]
, we show that periodicity can be recovered by extending
(A^{\delta},V)
into higher dimensions.

More precisely,
A^{\delta}
can be expressed as the restriction of an extension
A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
, defined in a
3
–dimensional space, which is periodic within a
2
–dimensional plane containing the edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
, and similarly for the potential
V
. This is closely related to the
quasiperiodic
behavior of
(A^{\delta},V)
in the
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
–direction, as explained in Remark
3.2
below.

We introduce the following extensions of
V
and
A^{\delta}
to functions on a three-dimensional domain:

\displaystyle\forall\;({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R},\quad V_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s)

\displaystyle:=V({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s)

\displaystyle:={\operatorname{Id}}-\delta\,\kappa\big(\delta{\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot({\bm{x}}+s{\mathbf{v}}_{2})\big)\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}.

(3.6)

Then,
A^{\delta}
is the restriction of
A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
to the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
,
i.e.

A^{\delta}({\bm{x}})=A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},0)
. We refer to
A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
as an
augmented
coefficient or an
extension / lift
of
A^{\delta}
, and similarly for
V_{\textnormal{aug}}
.

From its expression,
A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
interpolates smoothly and (for
\delta
small) slowly, via the domain wall
\kappa
, between the functions
({\bm{x}},s)\mapsto{\operatorname{Id}}+\delta\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}
and
({\bm{x}},s)\mapsto{\operatorname{Id}}-\delta\,a({\bm{x}})\,\sigma_{2}
, in the direction transverse to the two-dimensional interface

\Gamma:=\left\{({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\ /\ {\mathfrak{K}}_{2}\cdot({\bm{x}}+s{\mathbf{v}}_{2})=0\right\}.

(3.7)

Introduce the three-dimensional vectors:

{\mathbf{a}}_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\\
r\end{pmatrix},\quad{\mathbf{a}}_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\\
-1\end{pmatrix},\quad{\mathbf{a}}_{\perp}:=\begin{pmatrix}0_{2}\\
1\end{pmatrix}\ .

(3.8)

The set
\{{\mathbf{a}}_{1},{\mathbf{a}}_{2}\}
spans
\Gamma
, while the vector
{\mathbf{a}}_{\perp}
is transverse to
\Gamma
.

Our interest in
A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
lies in its
translation invariance
within the interface
\Gamma
. In contrast to
A^{\delta}
which is not necessarily translation-invariant along the edge
\mathbb{R}\,{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
, the relations (
3.5
) and the
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
–periodicity of
a(\cdot)
imply

\displaystyle\forall\;({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R},\quad A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{1},s+r)=A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s).

(3.9a)

On the other hand, the
{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
–periodicity of
a(\cdot)
implies that

\displaystyle\forall\;({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R},\quad A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{2},s-1)=A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s).

(3.9b)

In other words,
A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}
is invariant under translations by the vectors
{\mathbf{a}}_{1},{\mathbf{a}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which span the interface
\Gamma
. The relations (
3.9a
,
3.9b
) are also satisfied by
V_{\textnormal{aug}}
.

If
r=b_{1}/a_{1}
is rational, then (
3.9a
,
3.9b
) imply that
{\bm{x}}\mapsto A^{\delta}_{\textnormal{aug}}({\bm{x}},s)
is
\mathbb{Z}(a_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathfrak{v}}_{1})
–periodic for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, in particular for
s=0
. This is expected for rational edges, since it corresponds to (
3.2
), up to a renormalization of
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}
